Original: select     w_state   ,i_item_id   ,sum(case when (cast(d_date as date) < cast ('2001-04-07' as date))  		then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0) else 0 end) as sales_before   ,sum(case when (cast(d_date as date) >= cast ('2001-04-07' as date))  		then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0) else 0 end) as sales_after  from    catalog_sales left outer join catalog_returns on        (cs_order_number = cr_order_number         and cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk)   ,warehouse   ,item   ,date_dim  where  i_item_sk          = cs_item_sk  and cs_warehouse_sk    = w_warehouse_sk  and cs_sold_date_sk    = d_date_sk  and d_date between  (cast ('2001-04-07' as date) - interval '30' day)                 and (cast ('2001-04-07' as date) + interval '30' day)   and i_category  = 'Sports'  and i_manager_id between 61 and 100  and cs_wholesale_cost between 14 and 33  and cr_reason_sk = 40  group by     w_state,i_item_id  order by w_state,i_item_id limit 100;

Worker 1: Found optimized SQL
```sql
SELECT
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    SUM(CASE WHEN d.d_date < DATE '2001-04-07'
             THEN cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             ELSE 0 END) AS sales_before,
    SUM(CASE WHEN d.d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07'
             THEN cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
             ELSE 0 END) AS sales_after
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN catalog_returns cr
  ON cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
 AND cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
 AND cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
JOIN warehouse w
  ON cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '2001-03-08' AND DATE '2001-05-07'
  AND i.i_category       = 'Sports'
  AND i.i_manager_id     BETWEEN 61 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 14 AND 33
GROUP BY
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id
ORDER BY
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id
LIMIT 100;
```
</enhanced_sql>

</analysis>
Both rewrites correctly recognize that the `cr_reason_sk = 40` predicate in the WHERE clause effectively converts the LEFT OUTER JOIN into an INNER JOIN, and both push this condition into the JOIN's ON clause, enabling the optimizer to filter catalog_returns earlier. Both also simplify the redundant `cast(d_date as date)` expressions and precompute the date range boundaries (`2001-03-08` to `2001-05-07`), which removes per-row casting and aids index usage on d_date. Statement 0 uses explicit table aliases consistently throughout all column references (e.g., `w.w_state`, `i.i_item_id`), improving readability and eliminating any ambiguity, and its formatting is clean and well-structured. Statement 1 is functionally equivalent but leaves some columns unqualified. Since they are semantically identical in cost, the choice rests on clarity and robustness against future schema changes, which favors statement 0.

A careful, node-by-node comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs (original, rewritten, and enhanced) reveals that all three plans are structurally identical. The only textual differences are cosmetic: the rewritten and enhanced versions use short table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w) instead of full table names, and the date literals are expressed as `date` casts rather than `timestamp without time zone` comparisons. Neither change affects the optimizer's decisions — the same scan methods, join order, join algorithms, aggregate strategy, worker count, row estimates, widths, and cost figures appear in all three plans. Consequently, every metric requested for the report shows zero delta, and the analysis below documents that equivalence rather than an actual optimization gain.
</analysis>

**1. Cost Efficiency**
- **Overall cost change percentage: 0%.** All three plans report identical cost boundaries: startup cost 36171.37 and total cost 36171.68 at the Limit node, with the innermost Partial GroupAggregate at 35171.36..35171.40. The rewrite and enhancement introduced no cost change whatsoever.
- **Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0%.** The most expensive node in all three plans is the inner Nested Loop joining date_dim and catalog_sales (cost=0.43..34479.28), which dominates the plan's total cost. Its cost is unchanged across all three versions.

**2. Plan Characteristics**
- **Scan type transitions: none.** All three plans use a Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim combined with Index Scans on idx_catalog_sales_sold_date, idx_item_sk, catalog_returns_pkey, and warehouse_pkey. No scan method was upgraded or downgraded.
- **Join algorithm refinements: none.** All three plans use the same four-level Nested Loop join hierarchy (date_dim → catalog_sales → item → catalog_returns → warehouse) with identical join order and identical index conditions on each join.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting / reduction of intermediate results: none.** Each plan retains the same Sort node (Sort Key: w_state, i_item_id, cost=35171.36..35171.36) feeding the Partial GroupAggregate, and the estimated row counts (1 row at the aggregate, 85/1998/37 rows at intermediate joins, width=45→93) are identical in all three versions. No intermediate result set shrank.

**3. Resource Utilization**
- **Memory usage (Hash/Buffer nodes): unchanged.** None of the plans contain Hash Join or Hash Aggregate nodes, so there is no hash memory to compare; the memory-relevant structures (the Sort node and per-loop index probes) are byte-for-byte identical, with the same estimated row widths (45 at the join level, 93 at the aggregate output).
- **Worker count adjustment: none.** All three plans plan exactly 1 parallel worker (Workers Planned: 1) for the Gather Merge over the date_dim scan; no worker count was increased or reduced.

**4. Other Improvements**
- The only observable differences between the original and the rewritten/enhanced plans are **readability improvements**: the use of concise table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w) and the replacement of timestamp-style date comparisons with explicit `::date` casts. These changes do not alter the query's semantics as the optimizer sees them — the planner produces the identical plan, cost, and row estimates in all cases.
- **Conclusion:** the rewriting/enhancement was a no-op from a performance perspective. Because the original plan already relied on a well-chosen index-driven Nested Loop strategy with parallel scan on the date filter, the rewrite neither degraded nor improved the plan. To achieve actual gains on this query shape, one would need to look beyond textual rewriting — e.g., verifying index usefulness for the date range selectivity on date_dim, considering a composite index on catalog_sales (cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost), or adding statistics — rather than expecting further improvement from the current rewrite.

Another idea: Use `FILTER (WHERE d_date < DATE '2001-04-07')` instead of `CASE WHEN`. This is more readable and can be more efficient. The original uses `SUM(CASE WHEN ... THEN ... ELSE 0 END)`. In PostgreSQL, `SUM(...) FILTER (WHERE ...)` is often more efficient because it doesn't require evaluating the ELSE 0 for every row? Actually, `CASE` and `FILTER` are similar in performance. But `FILTER` can be faster because it only aggregates rows that meet the condition, avoiding the overhead of adding 0 for non-matching rows. For two conditional sums, `FILTER` might be slightly more efficient. Let's consider rewriting:
SELECT w_state, i_item_id,
       SUM(cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_refunded_cash,0)) FILTER (WHERE d_date < DATE '2001-04-07') AS sales_before,
       SUM(cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_refunded_cash,0)) FILTER (WHERE d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07') AS sales_after
FROM ...
GROUP BY ...
This is equivalent and might be slightly more efficient because it avoids the `CASE` expression and the `ELSE 0`. It can reduce CPU. But will it change the plan? Possibly not, but it's a valid rewrite. The previous report didn't mention FILTER. This could be a new optimization. Let's check if PostgreSQL supports FILTER. Yes, since 9.4. The explain shows PostgreSQL. So we can propose this as a rewrite with a small performance gain. However, the mission says "rewrite score" and "most efficient way". We can assign a score. But the previous report's termination criteria said "execution time is unchanged (not < original), the optimization does not meet the termination criteria." If we use FILTER, it might slightly improve execution time. But we don't have execution times. We can reason that it's more efficient.

Also, note that the original query uses `cast(d_date as date)`. The previous rewrite removed that. We can include that. Also use `INNER JOIN` and move `cr_reason_sk = 40` to ON. And use `DATE` literals. And use `FILTER`. That would be a new, more efficient rewrite.

Let's draft the optimized SQL:
```sql
SELECT
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)) FILTER (WHERE d.d_date < DATE '2001-04-07') AS sales_before,
    SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)) FILTER (WHERE d.d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07') AS sales_after
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN catalog_returns cr
  ON cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
 AND cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
 AND cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
JOIN warehouse w
  ON cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '2001-03-08' AND DATE '2001-05-07'
  AND i.i_category       = 'Sports'
  AND i.i_manager_id     BETWEEN 61 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 14 AND 33
GROUP BY
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id
ORDER BY
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id
LIMIT 100;
```
This uses FILTER. Is it equivalent? Yes, because for rows where d_date < date, the FILTER includes them in sales_before and excludes from

Actually, `SUM(expr) FILTER (WHERE cond)` only evaluates `expr` for rows where `cond` is true. The original `CASE` evaluates `expr` for every row? Actually, `CASE WHEN cond THEN expr ELSE 0 END` evaluates `expr` only when cond is true. So both evaluate expr only for matching rows. The difference is that the original still processes the ELSE 0 for non-matching rows, adding 0 to the sum. FILTER skips them entirely. This can be faster for sparse conditions. But here about half rows match each? The date range is 61 days, split at 2001-04-07. There are 30 days before and 31 days after. So roughly half rows match each condition. </produced_sql>
```sql
select w.w_state, i.i_item_id,
       sum(case when d.d_date < date '2001-04-07'
                then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
                else 0 end) as sales_before,
       sum(case when d.d_date >= date '2001-04-07'
                then cs.cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0)
                else 0 end) as sales_after
from catalog_sales cs
join catalog_returns cr
  on cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
 and cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
 and cr.cr_reason_sk = 40
join item i
  on cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
join date_dim d
  on cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
join warehouse w
  on cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
where d.d_date between date '2001-03-08' and date '2001-05-07'
  and i.i_category = 'Sports'
  and i.i_manager_id between 61 and 100
  and cs.cs_wholesale_cost between 14 and 33
group by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
order by w.w_state, i.i_item_id
limit 100;
```

</analysis>
The rewrite preserves equivalence: the LEFT JOIN plus `cr_reason_sk = 40` in WHERE forces an inner-join semantics, so moving the filter into the ON clause with an INNER JOIN yields identical results. Given data statistics — catalog_returns (2.1M) is far smaller than catalog_sales (14.4M) and the (cr_item_sk, cr_order_number) key lookups return at most one row per sale — pre-aggregating returns or building CTEs offers no benefit and only adds materialization overhead; the optimizer already uses an index nested loop for returns. The date range of 61 days (out of 73049 dates) is very selective and correctly drives the date_dim → catalog_sales index path, so forcing a different join order via CTEs would likely be harmful. Folding the date literals makes the d_date predicate sargable. The remaining plan (Finalize/Partial GroupAggregate with Sort for ORDER BY ... LIMIT 100) is already the natural cheapest plan, so no further structural rewrite (CTE creation, return pre-aggregation, early filtering restructure) would improve it. 
</analysis>

Statement 0 correctly recognizes that the original LEFT OUTER JOIN with `cr_reason_sk = 40` in the WHERE clause is effectively an inner join, so converting to an inner JOIN with the reason filter in the join condition preserves semantics while simplifying the plan. It keeps the date logic simple and safe using the date_dim join on `d_date`, avoids fragile assumptions, and preserves all filter/group/order/limit semantics exactly.

Statement 1 introduces a CTE with sk-derived boundaries and `FILTER` clauses, but relies on the assumption that `d_date_sk` values are monotonically aligned with `d_date`, and its MIN/MAX sk computation is fragile (e.g., the split_sk/end_sk derivation could be wrong if the table isn't strictly ordered or if gap-free sks can't be assumed). It adds complexity and correctness risk with little gain over simply filtering on `d.d_date`, which the optimizer can already convert efficiently.

Therefore statement 0 is the best choice.

1. The original and rewritten plans are structurally and numerically identical — every node has the same cost, row estimate, and width. The only textual differences are the introduction of table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w) and the rewrite of the date literals from timestamp constants to date constants with an implicit cast. Neither change affects the plan shape or the planner's cost model.

3. Key cost figures across all three plans: total top-level cost 36171.37..36171.68; the most expensive node is the innermost Nested Loop driving the catalog_sales index scan at cost 0.43..34479.28, which accounts for roughly 95% of the total cost. This node's cost is unchanged in all three plans.

4. Scan and join strategies are stable: Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim, Index Scans on catalog_sales (via idx_catalog_sales_sold_date), item, catalog_returns (pkey), and warehouse, all connected via Nested Loop joins. No Hash or Merge nodes exist, so no memory-heavy hash tables or explicit sort spillover is involved beyond the small Sort feeding the Partial GroupAggregate.

5. Parallelism: Workers Planned = 1 in all three plans, with a Gather Merge on top — no change in worker allocation.

Conclusion: the rewrite and enhancement did not materially change the execution plan; any performance gain would come only from potential literal-type normalization benefits (date vs. timestamp comparison), not from plan restructuring. The plan is already dominated by index-driven nested loops, which is efficient for this selective query.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: none. All three plans use the same access paths — a Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim and Index Scans on catalog_sales, item, catalog_returns, and warehouse. The rewrite did not convert any Seq Scan into an Index Scan or vice versa. The date_dim Seq Scan remains because the date-range predicate is only mildly selective relative to the table size.

A single Sort node (cost 35171.36) feeding the Partial GroupAggregate persists in all plans, and the estimated intermediate row counts (37 → 1998 → 85 → 1) are identical throughout. Widths are also identical, so no projection pruning was achieved by the rewrite.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: no change. Since there are no Hash nodes in any plan, there is no hash memory footprint to reduce. The only memory consumers are the small Sort node and the Gather Merge buffer, both unchanged.
- Worker count: unchanged. All three plans schedule 1 parallel worker for the date_dim scan with a Gather Merge above the Partial GroupAggregate. The enhancement did not justify additional workers.

Then provide rewritten SQL. Ensure executability, equivalence, efficiency, readability. Use PostgreSQL syntax. We'll use `DATE '2001-04-07'` etc. Use `FILTER` with `COALESCE`. Let's check equivalence:

Original:
```
sum(case when d_date < '2001-04-07' then cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0) else 0 end) as sales_before
```
If no rows match before for a group, sum of zeros? Actually if there are rows in group but none before, then for each row CASE returns 0, sum = 0. If there are no rows at all in group? Group only exists if at least one row matches join/filters. So there will be at least one row. So sales_before will be 0 if no before rows. With FILTER: `COALESCE(SUM(cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_refunded_cash,0)) FILTER (WHERE d_date < DATE '2001-04-07'), 0)`. If no before rows, SUM returns null, COALESCE gives 0. If there are before rows, SUM returns sum. Equivalent. Similarly for after.

But note: The original uses `sum(case when cond then expr else 0 end)`. If cond is true, it adds expr. If expr is null? `cs_sales_price - coalesce(cr_refunded_cash,0)` is null only if cs_sales_price is null. In that case, CASE returns null? Actually CASE WHEN cond THEN expr ELSE 0 END: if cond true, returns expr (which could be null). SUM ignores nulls. So null sales price is ignored. In our FILTER version, `SUM(expr) FILTER (WHERE cond)` also ignores nulls. So equivalent. If cond false, original returns 0, which is added to sum. But adding 0 doesn't affect sum. So ignoring rows where cond false is equivalent. So FILTER is equivalent.

We need to be careful with grouping: The original `sum(case when ... else 0 end)` for sales_before will be 0 if no rows match before, even if there are rows after. Our COALESCE ensures 0. Good.

Let's write SQL:

```sql
SELECT
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0))
             FILTER (WHERE d.d_date < DATE '2001-04-07'), 0) AS sales_before,
    COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0))
             FILTER (WHERE d.d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07'), 0) AS sales_after
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN catalog_returns cr
    ON cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
   AND cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
   AND cr.cr_reason_sk = 40
JOIN item i
    ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d
    ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
JOIN warehouse w
    ON cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '2001-03-08' AND DATE '2001-05-07'
  AND i.i_category = 'Sports'
  AND i.i_manager_id BETWEEN 61 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 14 AND 33
GROUP BY w.w_state, i.i_item_id
ORDER BY w.w_state, i.i_item_id
LIMIT 100;
```

Check: Original had `cr_reason_sk = 40` in WHERE, which null-rejects left join. So inner join is equivalent. Moving to ON with inner join is equivalent. The original left join condition was `catalog_sales left outer join catalog_returns on (cs_order_number = cr_order_number and cs_item_sk = cr_item_sk)`. Then WHERE `

</analysis>
The rewritten query folds the constant date arithmetic into literal dates (2001-03-08 to 2001-05-07) and removes redundant casts on d_date, which is already of date type. The implicit comma joins are converted to explicit INNER JOINs, and the cr_reason_sk = 40 predicate is moved into the JOIN ON clause — semantically identical to the original because the WHERE clause null-rejected the LEFT JOIN anyway.

For the aggregation, the two SUM(CASE ...) expressions are replaced with SUM(...) FILTER (WHERE ...) wrapped in COALESCE(..., 0). This is equivalent: rows failing the filter contribute 0 in the original CASE formulation (which does not change the sum), and the COALESCE handles the case where no rows match a period within a group (SUM over an empty set returns NULL, mapped to 0 as in the original).

Regarding data statistics: catalog_sales (14M rows) restricted by a 60-day date range yields roughly 100K+ rows; the item filters (i_category = 'Sports' AND i_manager_id BETWEEN 61 AND 100) are selective on the 102K-item dimension, and catalog_returns (2.1M rows) is reached via primary-key lookups on (cr_item_sk, cr_order_number). Starting from catalog_returns filtered by cr_reason_sk = 40 (~48K rows) would require ~48K sales lookups, whereas the current plan first reduces sales via the date range and item filters (to a few thousand rows) before probing returns — so the existing join order is preferred and no CTE materialization is warranted. An optional physical optimization is an index on catalog_returns (cr_reason_sk, cr_order_number, cr_item_sk) INCLUDE (cr_refunded_cash), but it is not required by the query rewrite itself.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
SELECT
    w.w_state,
    i.i_item_id,
    COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0))
             FILTER (WHERE d.d_date < DATE '2001-04-07'), 0) AS sales_before,
    COALESCE(SUM(cs.cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr.cr_refunded_cash, 0))
             FILTER (WHERE d.d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07'), 0) AS sales_after
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN catalog_returns cr
    ON cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
   AND cs.cs_item_sk      = cr.cr_item_sk
   AND cr.cr_reason_sk    = 40
JOIN item i
    ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d
    ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
JOIN warehouse w
    ON cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '2001-03-08' AND DATE '2001-05-07'
  AND i.i_category = 'Sports'
  AND i.i_manager_id BETWEEN 61 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 14 AND 33
GROUP BY w.w_state, i.i_item_id
ORDER BY w.w_state, i.i_item_id
LIMIT 100;
```

Both rewrites correctly recognize that the LEFT JOIN with the `cr_reason_sk = 40` filter can be converted to an INNER JOIN with the predicate pushed into the join condition, and both precompute the date literals. The key difference is the aggregation style: ID 1 uses `SUM(...) FILTER (WHERE ...)`, which is PostgreSQL-specific syntax and not portable. ID 0 uses the standard `SUM(CASE WHEN ...)` form, which is equivalent in semantics, works across virtually all engines, and typically optimizes to a single conditional aggregation pass. Therefore ID 0 is the best choice.

## selected Worker 0##
## Selected Rewritten SQL: SELECT w_state, i_item_id,
       SUM(CASE WHEN d_date < DATE '2001-04-07' THEN cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_refunded_cash,0) ELSE 0 END) AS sales_before,
       SUM(CASE WHEN d_date >= DATE '2001-04-07' THEN cs_sales_price - COALESCE(cr_refunded_cash,0) ELSE 0 END) AS sales_after
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN catalog_returns cr
  ON cs.cs_order_number = cr.cr_order_number
 AND cs.cs_item_sk = cr.cr_item_sk
 AND cr.cr_reason_sk = 40
JOIN item i
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
JOIN warehouse w
  ON cs.cs_warehouse_sk = w.w_warehouse_sk
WHERE d.d_date BETWEEN DATE '2001-03-08' AND DATE '2001-05-07'
  AND i.i_category = 'Sports'
  AND i.i_manager_id BETWEEN 61 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 14 AND 33
GROUP BY w_state, i_item_id
ORDER BY w_state, i_item_id
LIMIT 100; ##

Moved cr_reason_sk = 40 into the JOIN ON clause and converted to an explicit INNER JOIN, letting the optimizer choose the best join order.'}, {'group': 'Query_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Converted the legacy comma joins to explicit ANSI INNER JOINs for better readability and join-order clarity, with aliases for all tables.'}, {'group': 'Constant_folding', 'produced_suggestion': "Pre-computed the date literals (cast('2001-04-07' as date) - interval '30' day → DATE '2001-03-08', + 30 days → DATE '2001-05-07') to aid sargability on d_date/cs_sold_date_sk."}] ##

Comparing the three EXPLAIN plans line by line, the most important finding is that all three plans are structurally and cost-wise identical. The rewrite and enhanced rewrite did not change the physical plan selected by the optimizer: every node type, join order, join algorithm, cost estimate, row estimate, width, and worker count is exactly the same across the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans. The total plan cost remains 36171.37..36171.68 in all three cases, and the most expensive node (the deep Nested Loop chain rooted at the Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim, cost=0.43..34479.28) is likewise unchanged.

The differences between the plans are purely cosmetic / logical-level:
1. The rewritten and enhanced versions introduce short table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w), which improve readability but have no effect on the plan.
2. The date filter literals were simplified from timestamp literals ('2001-03-08 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone) to date literals ('2001-03-08'::date). The optimizer implicitly casts these to the column type, so the filter semantics, estimated rows (37), and cost (0.00..2068.55) are identical.

Because the query shape (TPC-DS-like join of date_dim → catalog_sales → item → catalog_returns → warehouse with aggregation on w_state, i_item_id) was not fundamentally restructured, no plan-shape improvement materialized.

Key structural observations that remain in all three plans (i.e., potential targets for future tuning):
- The innermost join still begins with a Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim with a highly selective date-range filter (only ~37 rows estimated), meaning an index on d_date could convert this to an Index Scan and avoid scanning the whole dimension table.
- The plan still contains an explicit Sort node feeding the Partial GroupAggregate (cost=35171.36), so there is an opportunity to eliminate sorting via an index on (w_state, i_item_id) or by enabling hash aggregation.
- Only 1 parallel worker is planned, so parallelism headroom exists.
- The catalog_sales access is a non-covering Index Scan with an additional filter on cs_wholesale_cost; a composite index on (cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost) could reduce heap fetches.

Since all numeric values are identical, every cost-change percentage is 0%.

- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0%. The dominant node remains the Nested Loop subtree (cost=0.43..34479.28) whose driving input is the Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim; its startup/total cost and row estimates (1998 rows) are unchanged in all three versions.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: None. The date_dim access remains a Parallel Seq Scan with a date-range filter in all three plans. No transition to Index Scan occurred. The other scans (catalog_sales, item, catalog_returns, warehouse) remain Index Scans using the same indexes (idx_catalog_sales_sold_date, idx_item_sk, catalog_returns_pkey, warehouse_pkey) with identical index conditions and filters.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. All three plans use the same four-level Nested Loop chain with the same join order; no switch to Hash Join or Merge Join occurred.
- Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction: Not achieved. The Sort node feeding the Partial GroupAggregate (Sort Key: w_state, i_item_id; cost=35171.36..35171.36, rows=1) is present in all three plans. Estimated intermediate row counts and widths (rows=1, width=45 at the Sort input) are unchanged.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: Unchanged. No Hash nodes exist in any plan (join strategy is nested-loop based), so no Hash memory is allocated. The explicit Sort node remains in all three plans, implying identical sorting memory/workfile requirements. Buffer behavior is therefore expected to be equivalent.
- Worker count: Unchanged. All three plans use Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim with Workers Planned: 1 and a Gather Merge above the Partial GroupAggregate. No adjustment in parallelism was made or gained.

4. Other Improvements
- Readability/maintainability: The rewritten and enhanced queries introduce concise table aliases (d, cs, i, cr, w), making the plan output and query text easier to read. This is a cosmetic improvement with no runtime effect.
- Literal normalization: The rewritten/enhanced versions express the date predicates as plain date literals ('2001-03-08'::date, '2001-05-07'::date) instead of explicit timestamp literals. The optimizer casts them identically, so selectivity, rows (37), and cost are unchanged, but the predicate is cleaner and less error-prone in the query text.
- Plan stability: All three queries compile to the identical physical plan, indicating the rewrites did not perturb the optimizer's decisions — a neutral but safe outcome.

Summary and recommendations: The rewrite and enhanced rewrite were semantically equivalent at the plan level and yielded 0% cost improvement. The main remaining bottlenecks are (a) the Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim despite a highly selective date range — adding an index on d_date could convert it to an Index Scan; (b) the explicit Sort before GroupAggregate — an index supporting (w_state, i_item_id) ordering or hash aggregation could remove the sort; (c) limited parallelism (1 worker) on a plan dominated by a costly nested-loop chain; and (d) a non-covering index scan on catalog_sales with an extra cs_wholesale_cost filter — a composite index including cs_wholesale_cost could reduce I/O. These are the recommended levers for actual cost reduction in future optimization rounds.
